<div class="ltx_page_main">

<div class="ltx_page_content">

# LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation

<div class="ltx_authors">

<span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author"> <span class="ltx_personname">
Philippe Laban  <sup>♢</sup>   Hiroaki Hayashi<sup>$`*`$♣</sup>   Yingbo
Zhou<sup>♣</sup>   Jennifer Neville<sup>♢</sup>  
<sup>♢</sup>Microsoft Research   <sup>♣</sup>Salesforce Research  
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">{plaban,jenneville}@microsoft.com</span>  
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">{hiroakihayashi,yingbo.zhou}@salesforce.com</span>  
</span><span class="ltx_author_notes">Equal Contributions</span></span>

</div>

<div class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Large Language Models (LLMs) are conversational interfaces. As such,
LLMs have the potential to assist their users not only when they can
fully specify the task at hand, but also to help them define, explore,
and refine what they need through multi-turn conversational exchange.
Although analysis of LLM conversation logs has confirmed that
underspecification occurs frequently in user instructions, LLM
evaluation has predominantly focused on the single-turn, fully-specified
instruction setting. In this work, we perform large-scale simulation
experiments to compare LLM performance in single- and multi-turn
settings. Our experiments confirm that all the top open- and
closed-weight LLMs we test exhibit significantly lower performance in
multi-turn conversations than single-turn, with an average drop of 39%
across six generation tasks. Analysis of 200,000+ simulated
conversations decomposes the performance degradation into two
components: a minor loss in aptitude and a significant increase in
unreliability. We find that LLMs often make assumptions in early turns
and prematurely attempt to generate final solutions, on which they
overly rely. In simpler terms, we discover that
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">when LLMs take a wrong turn in a
conversation, they get lost and do not recover</span>.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1: </span>In
this work, we simulate single- and multi-turn conversations for six
generation tasks. The 15 LLMs we test perform much worse in multi-turn
settings (-35%) explained by some loss in aptitude, and large losses in
reliability. Aptitude is defined as performance in best-case
conversation simulation, and unreliability as the gap between best- and
worst-case performance. In short, we find that LLMs get lost in
multi-turn, underspecified conversation.</figcaption>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Today’s large language models (LLMs) function as conversational
interfaces (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, ChatGPT,
Gemini, Claude), enabling users to interact with the LLM through
multiple conversation turns. Such interaction promises to help users not
only when they know what they need (i.e., they can fully specify their
requirements in an instruction), but also when they don’t. In such
cases, users might start with an underspecified instruction and further
clarify their needs through turn interactions. Though studies of LLM
conversation logs have confirmed that underspecification in user
instructions is prevalent
\[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\], LLM systems are
typically evaluated in single-turn, fully-specified settings.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Even though a growing body of work proposes to evaluate LLMs in a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">multi-turn</span> fashion, we
identify in our review (Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Background and Related Work ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) that most prior work treats
the conversation as
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">episodic</span>: conversation
turns might relate to each other, but the conversation can effectively
be decomposed as an array of subtasks that can be evaluated in
isolation. We argue that episodic tasks move away from what is prevalent
in human conversation:
underspecification \[<a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref">91</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In this work, we close this gap by creating a simulation environment for
multi-turn underspecified conversations – sharded simulation – that
leverages existing instructions from high-quality single-turn
benchmarks. At a high level, the sharding process we propose transforms
existing single-turn instructions into
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">sharded instructions</span>, a
set of smaller instructions that jointly deliver the same information as
the original instruction. Sharded simulation then ensures that each turn
of conversation reveals at most one shard of information per
conversation turn, enforcing that the instruction is gradually revealed
through the conversation.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

On the set of tasks that we experimented on, we observed that models
engaged in multi-turn underspecified conversations achieved an average
performance of 65%–a 25-point drop from single-turn performances of 90%
when they receive the entire instruction at the beginning of the
conversation. Notably, we observe this drop in performance even in
two-turn conversations, and across all LLMs we test, from small
open-weights (LLama3.1-8B-Instruct) to state-of-the-art (Gemini 2.5
Pro).

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Furthermore, we decompose the performance degradation into two
components: (1) loss in aptitude, and (2) increase in unreliability. We
find that in single-turn settings, models with higher aptitude tend to
be more reliable (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>,
GPT-4.1, Gemini 2.5 Pro). On the other hand, all LLMs exhibit very high
unreliability in multi-turn settings, regardless of aptitude. We refer
to this as the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">lost in
conversation phenomenon</span>: when LLMs take a wrong turn in
multi-turn conversation, they get lost and do not recover.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We investigate several explanations for this effect and show that the
LLMs tend to (1) generate overly verbose responses, leading them to (2)
propose final solutions prematurely in conversation, (3) make incorrect
assumptions about underspecified details, and (4) rely too heavily on
previous (incorrect) answer attempts.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Our findings highlight a gap between how LLMs are used in practice and
how the models are being evaluated. Ubiquitous performance degradation
over multi-turn interactions is likely a reason for low uptake of AI
systems \[<a href="#bib.bib73" class="ltx_ref">73</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">4</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>\], particularly with novice
users who are less skilled at providing complete, detailed instructions
from the onset of conversation
\[<a href="#bib.bib87" class="ltx_ref">87</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S1.p8" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The rest of the paper is structured as follows:
Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Background and Related Work ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> situates our work with respect
to prior work on multi-turn evaluation. In
Section <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, we describe the simulation
environment we built for both single- and multi-turn conversations on a
diverse set of generation tasks. We introduce the six tasks and the
metrics we use to evaluate the aptitude and reliability of models in
Section <a href="#S4.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.1 Task Selection ‣ 4 Task and Metric Selection ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></a>.
Sections <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref"
title="5 Simulation Scale and Parameters ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>-<a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref"
title="6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> define our main experiment
involving 15 LLMs, and analyze the main findings. Finally, the
Implications section (Section <a href="#S7" class="ltx_ref"
title="7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>) discusses the ramifications
of the work, from the perspective of organizations that are building
LLM-based conversation products, to that of end-users of the LLM-based
systems. We provide actionable recommendations based on small-scale
experiments and make a concrete call-to-action to LLM builders, urging
them to prioritize multi-turn reliability in conjunction with aptitude
in future model iterations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Background and Related Work

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Previous-generation language models (e.g., BART
\[<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>\], GPT-2
\[<a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>\], or T5
\[<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>\]) were not equipped to
handle multi-turn conversations, which led evaluation to focus on
single-turn tasks \[<a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">79</a>\].
Conversational AI was typically implemented as specialized systems that
leveraged language models as components
\[<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>\], and were evaluated
through human protocols \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>\], or competitions like
Amazon’s Alex Prize \[<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">67</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

As the meteoric rise of ChatGPT led to increased interest in multi-turn
evaluation, initial popular efforts such as
MT-bench \[<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref">89</a>\] leveraged
crowd-sourced annotations to evaluate LLM-as-a-judge ability. Follow-up
works expanded on MT-bench, for instance to include longer
conversations \[<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>\], increase evaluation
granularity \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>\], or to tackle
different aspects such as
naturalness \[<a href="#bib.bib72" class="ltx_ref">72</a>\] or tool
use \[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref">85</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib80" class="ltx_ref">80</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Crucially, such works typically simulate
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">episodic</span> conversations:
each turn in the conversation introduces a subtask that relates to
previous conversation turns, but can be evaluated in isolation. In this
work, we find that episodic tasks overestimate LLM performance in
multi-turn conversations (see Section <a href="#S7.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.3 Implications for NLP Practitioners ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.3</span></a>). In short, although
episodic tasks require some level of multi-turn context understanding,
they do not involve actively fusing the information to answer
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">underspecified</span> user
instructions. Underspecified user instructions are not only common in
real-world human-AI
communication \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\], but also
a natural tendency in conversations, termed “the principle of least
effort” \[<a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref">91</a>\]. We show that
underspecification in multi-turn conversations leads to large and
universal performance degradations: LLMs make early assumptions to fill
in for missing information, prematurely attempt to propose finalized
solutions, and have difficulty adapting and course-correcting when
provided with new information. We make underspecification the central
element of our evaluation setting.

</div>

<div id="S2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Multi-turn episodic evaluation is sometimes framed as a way to evaluate
multi-turn model capabilities with higher granularity. Categories of
subtasks (such as refinement, follow-up, expansion, etc.) allow for the
study of more specific LLM behavior
\[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">74</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>\]. According to such
framing, multi-turn tasks
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">differ</span> from single-turn
tasks and are not evaluated on the same set of tasks. We argue that this
framing is artificial and limits the scope of multi-turn evaluation,
restricting the direct comparison of multi-turn and single-turn
abilities of LLMs. In our work, we conduct both single-turn and
multi-turn conversation simulations on
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">a common set of tasks</span>:
controlled experiments that precisely allow us to identify performance
degradations from single- to multi-turn settings.

</div>

<div id="S2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Evaluating LLMs in multi-turn settings is a challenge because
conversational trajectories diverge far more than in a single-turn.
Thus, most previous studies have focused on classification or short-form
tasks, with more straightforward evaluation settings. However, the
predominant use cases for LLMs are generative in nature, both for
programming (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, coding
assistants) and natural language
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, writing,
summarizing) \[<a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref">88</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>\]. Long-form evaluation in
the multi-turn setting is therefore essential, as it assesses models’
ability to flexibly adapt and refine the response as the users provide
more information. In this work, we focus exclusively on generation tasks
that capture widely used scenarios in both programming and natural
language domains.

</div>

<div id="S2.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Scaling multi-turn experimentation requires simulating a user. Existing
studies implemented such user simulation in different ways: relying on
templates \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">68</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">39</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>\], using an LLM
\[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>\], involving human
annotators \[<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>\], or real users as part of a
study \[<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">67</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>\]. Although involving real
users leads to the most natural and realistic conversations, it comes at
the cost of scalability and reproducibility. In this work, we adopt an
LLM-based simulator to enable controlled flexibility and divergence.
Nevertheless, a fully automated simulation limits the scope of our
findings: the conversations we simulate are not representative of
human-AI conversations. We therefore frame the simulation as a tool to
study the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">LLM behavior</span> in
the multi-turn setting rather than user behavior. In addition, as
detailed in the Limitations Section
(Section <a href="#S9" class="ltx_ref"
title="9 Limitations ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a>), we argue that our simulation
framework is simplistic and idealized. For example, the conversations
are guaranteed to end with sufficient information to solve the tasks,
and the simulator limits unexpected behavior
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, derailing) that can
occur in real-world settings. We suggest these choices imply that
degradations observed in this work are most likely underestimates of
what occurs in real-world, underspecified multi-turn Human-AI
conversations. Appendix <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Related work on Underspecification ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a> introduces other related work
specifically focused on underspecified communication.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To assess LLM performance in multi-turn, underspecified conversation, we
develop a simulation environment that repurposes existing tasks from
single-turn benchmarks. First, we apply a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">sharding process</span> to
transform original fully-specified instructions into
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">sharded instructions</span>.
Second, we implement a sharding simulation environment that carries out
a multi-turn conversation based on a sharded instruction.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Sharding Process: From Fully-Specified to Sharded Instructions

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

An original, fully-specified instruction from GSM8K
\[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>\] and the equivalent
sharded instruction are listed in
Figure <a href="#S3.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 3.1 Sharding Process: From Fully-Specified to Sharded Instructions ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The original instruction is a single, long utterance that introduces all
the content at once: a high-level question
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, “How long will it
take \[…\]”), context, and conditions. The sharded instruction is
composed of <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">a set of
shards</span>, each introducing a single element from the original
instruction. More specifically, the first shard (Shard 1) of a sharded
instruction always introduces the high-level intent for the instruction,
and subsequent shards each provide clarification to the instruction.
Taken jointly, the set of shards reflects the same information provided
in the fully-specified instruction, with the information explicitly
divided across shards.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B Precise Definition of Sharded Instructions ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>, we provide a more precise and
mathematical definition of a sharded instruction in relation to the
original fully-specified instruction, and define five key properties a
sharded instruction must satisfy to be considered valid.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

As part of our work, we developed a semi-automatic sharding process to
scale the creation of sharded instructions. This process, described in
depth in Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>, ensured that the experiments
we carried out used sharded instructions that adhered to the properties
we defined.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S3.F2.sf1"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">
<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img

instruction used in single-turn conversation simulation, and (<a
href="#S3.F2.sf2" class="ltx_ref"

to simulate underspecified, multi-turn conversation.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Simulating Sharded Conversations

<figure id="S3.F3" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x2.png" id="S3.F3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="747"
height="203" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3:
</span>Sharded Conversation Simulation Diagram. The subject for the
simulation is highlighted in red.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Figure <a href="#S3.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 3.2 Simulating Sharded Conversations ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> depicts the process of
simulating a multi-turn, underspecified conversation based on a sharded
instruction. At a high-level, the conversation involves three parties:
the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">assistant</span> is the LLM
being evaluated in the simulation, the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">user</span> (simulated by an LLM)
who has access to the entirety of the sharded instruction and is in
charge of revealing shards during turns of the conversation, and the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">system</span> which categorizes and
evaluates assistant responses.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

On the first turn, the user simulator reveals the first shard of the
instruction (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>,
Shard 1) to the assistant, which then generates a free text response.
The system processes the assistant’s response into one of seven possible
response strategies:
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">clarification</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">refusal</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">hedging</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">interrogation</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">discussion</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">missing</span>, or
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">answer
attempt</span>,<span id="footnote1"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>See
Appendix <a href="#A7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix G Assistant Response Categorization ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">G</span></a> for the definition and the
example for each strategy.</span></span></span> based on Herlihy et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\]’s LLM response
categorization. If the assistant generates an answer attempt
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, proposing an
explicit, full-form solution), then the answer extractor component
determines the span that corresponds to the answer within the
assistant’s free-form response
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, code snippet,
number). This step is required because LLMs often pad answer attempts
with additional information, such as a natural-language explanation or a
follow-up question, which could hinder evaluation. Finally, the
extracted answer is scored by a task-specific evaluator function.
Subsequent turns follow a similar pattern: at each turn, the user
simulator reveals at most one shard of information, the assistant
responds freely, which gets evaluated if the response is classified as
an answer attempt. The conversation ends if one of two conditions is
met: (1) the task-evaluator assesses that an assistant answer attempt is
correct, or (2) if at the start of a new turn, the user simulator has
run out of shards to reveal in the conversation.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Preliminary experiments revealed that during simulation, evaluated
assistants often asked clarification questions that related to specific
shards of the instruction. As such, deciding which shard to reveal next
in the conversation (the role of the user simulator) is non-trivial, as
it should take into account the state of the conversation so far. We
instantiate the user simulator as a low-cost LLM (specifically,
GPT-4o-mini) that has access to the entire sharded instruction and the
state of the conversation so far, tasking it with deciding the next
shard to reveal that fits most naturally in the ongoing simulated
conversation. The user simulator is also tasked with rephrasing the
shard to fit naturally within the conversation without modifying its
informational content. See Appendix <a href="#A10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix J Example Simulated Conversation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">J</span></a> for an example simulated
sharded conversation.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Besides user messages, the assistant receives a minimal system
instruction (before the first turn) that provides the necessary context
to accomplish the task (such as a database schema or a list of available
API tools). Importantly, the assistant is not explicitly informed that
it is participating in a multi-turn, underspecified conversation and is
not encouraged to pursue specific conversational strategies. Although
such additional instructions would likely alter model behavior, we argue
that such changes are not realistic, as such information is not
available a priori in practical settings. In summary, we provide no
information about the setting to the evaluated assistant model during
simulation, aiming to assess default model behavior.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Apart from the user simulator, the strategy classifier and answer
extractor components are also implemented with prompt-based GPT-4o-mini.
While the choice of LLM-based components in the simulator allows for
dynamic choices that provide a more realistic simulation, they also
unavoidably lead to simulation errors, which can affect the validity of
experiments. To understand the scope of simulation errors and their
effect on simulation validity, we conducted an in-depth manual
annotation of several hundred simulatesouthworth2023developingd
conversations. The annotation effort and its findings are detailed in
Appendix <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Inspection of Simulated Sharded Conversation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>. In summary, we found that
errors introduced by the user simulator, strategy classifier, or answer
extraction occurred in less than 5% of inspected conversations and that
these errors disfavored the assistant model in less than 2% of the
conversations. We believe the process described above can accurately
simulate multi-turn, underspecified conversations based on sharded
instructions, and we rely on it to simulate conversations for our
experiments.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.3 </span>Simulation Types

<figure id="S3.F4" class="ltx_figure ltx_align_floatright">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x3.png" id="S3.F4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="415" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4:
</span>Conversation simulation types based on sharded instructions. Once
an original fully-specified instruction (blue block) is sharded (set of
yellow blocks), the “shards” can be used to simulate single-turn (<span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>, <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>) or multi-turn (<span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>, <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span>, <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span>) conversations,
affecting the pace of information disclosure.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We leverage sharded instructions to simulate five types of single- or
multi-turn conversations, as illustrated in
Figure <a href="#S3.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ 3.3 Simulation Types ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>. We now introduce each one and
explain its purpose in our experiments.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/full.png"
id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Fully-specified</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

(short-form: <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>)
simulates single-turn, fully-specified conversations in which the
original instruction is provided to the LLM in the first turn. This
simulation type evaluates baseline model performance on the tasks.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/sharded.png"
id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="16" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

simulates multi-turn, underspecified conversations as outlined above.
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulations are
our primary tool to evaluate model performance in underspecified,
multi-turn conversations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/concat.png"
id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="15" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

simulates single-turn, fully-specified conversation based on the sharded
instruction. The shards are concatenated into a single instruction in
bullet-point form (with one shard per line), preceded by an instruction
to complete the task taking into account all bullet-points. The
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> simulation is a
logical mid-point between full and sharded, in which underspecification
is removed (like <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>)
but the rephrasing that occurred during instruction sharding is
preserved (like
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>).
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> is intended as a
verification baseline: a model that succeeds at both
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>, but not at
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>, struggles
specifically because of underspecification and the multi-turn nature of
the conversation, and not due to the rephrasing that occurred during the
sharding process, which may have led to information loss.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/recap.png"
id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="13" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

simulates a <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
conversation, and adds a final
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">recapitulation turn</span> which
restates all the shards of the instruction in a single turn, giving the
LLM one final attempt at responding.
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> is a combination
of the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
simulation followed by a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> turn, and is
explored as a method in Section <a href="#S7.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.1 Implications for System and Agent Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.1</span></a> to evaluate whether such a
conceptually simple agent-like intervention can mitigate the loss in
performance observed in
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> conversations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/snowball.png"
id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span>

<div id="S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

takes the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span>
simulation a step further, implementing turn-level recapitulation. At
each turn, the user simulator introduces a new shard, but also restates
all the shards that have been revealed so far in the conversation,
producing a <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">snowball</span>
effect as each turn reveals all the information from the previous turn,
plus one additional shard. The redundancy implemented in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span> simulation is
also explored as a method in Section <a href="#S7.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.1 Implications for System and Agent Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.1</span></a> to study whether turn-level
reminders help alleviate the need for LLMs to recall information across
multiple turns of context.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Task and Metric Selection

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Task Selection

<figure id="S4.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x4.png" id="S4.F5.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="503" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span>Six
sharded tasks included in our experiments. We purposefully include tasks
that involve generating programming and natural language. For each task,
an illustrative fully-specified instruction and its sharded counterpart.
We sharded 90-120 instructions based on high-quality datasets
(Instruction Origin), re-purposing existing evaluation.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We constructed sharded instructions for six tasks that we use in a
large-scale simulation experiment. For each task, we selected
instructions from one or two high-quality single-turn, fully-specified
benchmarks, and implemented a semi-automatic sharding process. The
process relied first on an LLM (GPT-4o) to propose and verify sharding
candidates, which were then reviewed and edited (when necessary) by the
authors of the work. The sharding process (outlined in detail in
Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>) allowed us to scale the
construction of sharded instruction corpora while ensuring validity of
the underlying instructions. For each task, we prepared 90-120 sharded
instructions (each paired with the original single-turn instructions),
which required between 1-4 hours of manual inspection and annotation.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We carefully selected popular and diverse generation tasks across
programming and non-programming use cases.
Figure <a href="#S4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ 4.1 Task Selection ‣ 4 Task and Metric Selection ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> provides an example of an
original and sharded instruction for each task, which we now introduce.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/python.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Code

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant must help the user write a function in the Python
programming language. The original instructions were sourced from the
HumanEval \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">10</a>\] and
LiveCodeBench \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\] datasets,
two popular benchmarks used to evaluate LLM programming aptitude.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/database.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Database

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant is provided with the schema of an SQL database and a user
query in natural language, and must produce an SQL query that retrieves
the requested information from the database (a.k.a., text-to-SQL). The
original instructions and databases were sourced from the popular Spider
dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">86</a>\].

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/apis.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Actions

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant is provided with a set of API (Application Programming
Interface) schemas, and a user instruction that requires API use, and
must generate the programmatic API commands that match the user request.
We sourced API schemas and user instructions from the Berkeley Function
Calling Leaderboard (BFCL)
\[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref">85</a>\], a popular benchmark
used to measure LLM ability at API function calling.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/math.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px4.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Math

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant is provided with an elementary math word problem, and must
perform a series of calculations using basic arithmetic operations to
reach a numerical answer. We sourced problems from the GSM8K dataset
\[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>\].

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/data2text.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px5.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Data-to-text

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant is provided tabular data and several elements of related
metadata, and must produce a caption (natural language sentence)
describing the underlying data. We leverage the ToTTo
\[<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">59</a>\] dataset to formulate
sharded instructions.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px6" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/summary.png"
id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px6.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square"
width="14" height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Summary

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px6.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The assistant receives a corpus of around twenty documents and a user
query, and must generate a summary with citations that addresses the
query based on the documents. We re-purpose the instructions from
Summary of a Haystack \[<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\].
The summary task is the only task we include that tests long-context
capabilities, with instructions spanning several tens of thousands of
tokens, which is known to deteriorate model performance
\[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px6.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

For each task, we reuse the metrics used in the original benchmarks.
More specifically, the first four tasks (Code, Database, Actions, and
Math) are evaluated for binary correctness, either by executing an
answer attempt (code, SQL query), or validating semantic equivalence to
a reference answer (API call, numerical answer). The last two tasks
(Data-to-Text and Summary) are
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">refinement tasks</span>, which
get scored on a continuous range (0-100). Data-to-text uses the BLEU
metric \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\], and Summary uses
a custom LLM-as-a-judge metric (“Joint Score”) built to measure
information coverage and attribution accuracy of the summary
\[<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\]. We map binary accuracy
in the range of 0-100 (0 = failure, 100 = success) so that all tasks
produce scores on a common scale, facilitating aggregation.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px6.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Appendix <a href="#A9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix I Task-specific Implementation details ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">I</span></a> lists implementation details
of the sharding process for each task, including the sample selection
process and any task-specific logic that was implemented to facilitate
reproducibility. Even though we intended for the six selected tasks to
be representative of a wide range of LLM use cases, we put effort into
making the sharding process efficient and reproducible, as we see the
process itself as a contribution of our work. We envision that future
LLM evaluation practitioners can shard their own dataset artifacts to
study LLM multi-turn behavior in more diverse and unique settings.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Metric Selection

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

LLMs employ a stochastic process to generate text. When setting LLM
generation parameters to their default
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, T=1.0), LLMs
generate many distinct responses for a fixed conversation state. We
leverage this property to conduct repeated simulations for a given
instruction and observe the variations that occur. Each simulation
yields a score $`S_{i}`$ ranging from 0-100 that assesses the level of
success of the LLM in completing the task by the end of the simulation.
Based on the set of scores $`S=\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}`$ obtained from
running $`N`$ simulations for an instruction, we define three metrics:
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">averaged performance</span>
($`\overline{P}`$), <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">aptitude</span>
($`A^{90}`$), and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">unreliability</span>
($`U_{10}^{90}`$):

</div>

<div class="ltx_logical-block">

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<div class="ltx_block ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:86.7pt;">

|     |                                                       |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                       
       ``` math                                               
       \overline{P}=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}S_{i}\big{/}N  
       ```                                                    |     |

</div>

<div class="ltx_block ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:130.1pt;">

|     |                                          |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                          
       ``` math                                  
       A^{90}=\operatorname{percentile}_{90}(S)  
       ```                                       |     |

</div>

<div class="ltx_block ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_middle"
style="width:208.1pt;">

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
U_{10}^{90}=\operatorname{percentile}_{90}(S)-\operatorname{percentile}_{10}(S).
``` |  |

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Average performance $`\overline{P}`$ is an unbiased estimate of a
model’s mean score on an instruction in a given simulation type.
Aptitude $`A^{90}`$ is an estimate of a model’s 90th percentile score on
a given instruction, a best-case metric that estimates scores obtained
in the top 10% of simulations conducted. Unreliability is an
interpercentile range estimate, between the 90th and 10th percentile
estimates, measuring the gap between best-case and worst-case
simulations, giving a sense of
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">level of degradation</span> that
occurs in response quality due to stochasticity in the LLM.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each of the metrics is computed on a per-instruction basis and can be
averaged across a corpus of instructions to obtain corpus-level metrics.
In the rest of the paper, we refer to reliability and unreliability
interchangeably, with reliability defined as
$`R_{10}^{90}=100-U_{10}^{90}`$. We also simplify the notations to $`A`$
for aptitude and $`U`$ for unreliability, though the metrics can be
generalized to other percentile thresholds
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>, $`A^{80}`$ or
$`U_{5}^{95}`$).

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In Appendix <a href="#A5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix E Concrete Example of Loss in Aptitude vs. Reliability ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E</span></a>, we go over a concrete example
of how an average degradation in performance ($`\overline{P}`$) from 90%
to 60% could be due to a loss in aptitude, reliability, or a
combination. Finally, Figure <a href="#S6.F6.sf1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(a)</span></a> visually connects the
aptitude and unreliability metrics to score box-plot visualizations. In
summary, the height of the upper whisker of the box plot represents
aptitude (A), and the distance between the upper and lower whiskers of
the plot represents Unreliability (U).

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Simulation Scale and Parameters

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In the main simulation experiment, we leveraged the totality of
instructions we sharded across six tasks (a total of 600 instructions),
and simulated conversations across three types:
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/full.png" id="S5.p1.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> 
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>,
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/concat.png" id="S5.p1.m2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="15" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> 
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>, and
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/sharded.png" id="S5.p1.m3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="16" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> 
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>. We
experimented with 15 LLMs, running $`N=10`$ simulations for each pair of
model and simulation type, totaling more than 200,000 simulated
conversations. All simulations were conducted with a default temperature
of $`T=1`$, however, we conducted a supplementary experiment
(Section <a href="#S7.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.2 Implications for LLM Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.2</span></a>) that explores the effect of
temperature on aptitude and reliability.

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Although simulating ten conversations for each
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">(LLM, instruction, simulation
type)</span> increases experimental costs ten-fold, it allows us to not
only measure averaged performance ($`\overline{P}`$) more accurately,
but also study aptitude and reliability of LLM systems in depth in
Section <a href="#S6.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.2</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S5.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We selected a total of 15 LLMs from eight model families:
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/openai.png" id="S5.p3.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> OpenAI (GPT-4o-mini, GPT-4o
\[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>\], o3
\[<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a>\], and GPT-4.1),
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/claude.png" id="S5.p3.m2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Anthropic (Claude 3 Haiku,
Claude 3.7 Sonnet), Google’s
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/gemini.png" id="S5.p3.m3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Gemini (Gemini 2.5 Flash,
Gemini 2.5 Pro) \[<a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">75</a>\], Meta’s
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/llama.png" id="S5.p3.m4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Llama (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct,
Llama3.3-70B-Instruct, Llama 4 Scout)
\[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\],
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/ai2.png" id="S5.p3.m5.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="12" height="12"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> AI2 OLMo-2-13B
\[<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">56</a>\],
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/microsoft.png" id="S5.p3.m6.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="12" height="12"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Microsoft Phi-4
\[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\],
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/deepseek.png" id="S5.p3.m7.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Deepseek-R1
\[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\], and
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/cohere.png" id="S5.p3.m8.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="12" height="12"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Cohere Command-A
\[<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">15</a>\]. This selection
prioritizes the evaluation of state-of-the-art models, including both
small (8B) and large models (300B+). We purposefully include both open-
and closed-weights models, as well as two reasoning models (o3, R1) to
study the effect additional thinking (test-time compute) has on
multi-turn conversation capability. Details on model versioning and
access are listed in Appendix <a href="#A8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix H Model Access ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">H</span></a>. We estimate the total cost of
conducting simulations to be around \$5,000.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.1 </span>Average Performance Findings

<div id="S6.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Table <a href="#S6.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> summarizes results from the
simulation. At a high level, <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">every
model sees its performance degrade on every task when comparing
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> performance,
with an average degradation of -39%</span>. We name this phenomenon
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Lost in Conversation</span>:
models that achieve stellar (90%+) performance in the lab-like setting
of fully-specified, single-turn conversation struggle on
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">the exact same tasks</span> in a
more realistic setting when the conversation is underspecified and
multi-turn.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In comparison, models perform roughly equivalently in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> setting, with
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> performance
averaging 95.1% of the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> counterpart. This
implies that the loss in performance for
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> is not
explained by potential loss of information in sharded instructions, as
such a loss would be reflected in lower
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> performance. We
observe that smaller models (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct, OLMo-2-13B, Claude 3
Haiku) have more pronounced
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> degradations
(86-92), and interpret this as indicating that smaller models struggle
to generalize as well as larger models: benign rephrasing affects
performance more than for larger, more robust models. This lack of
robustness to paraphrasing can be observed visually in
Table <a href="#S6.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>:
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> degradation (red
background) is more pronounced in the top rows (weaker models) than the
bottom rows (stronger models).

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The last column of the Table (<span class="ltx_text"
style="position:relative; bottom:2.2pt;">
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/sharded.png"
id="S6.SS1.p3.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="16"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span></span>
<span class="ltx_text"
style="position:relative; bottom:-2.2pt;">/</span>
<span class="ltx_text" style="position:relative; bottom:-2.2pt;">
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/full.png" id="S6.SS1.p3.m2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span></span>) aggregates
performance degradation across the six tasks, summarizing the magnitude
of the Lost in Conversation effect for each model. Surprisingly,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">more performant models (Claude 3.7
Sonnet, Gemini 2.5, GPT-4.1) get equally lost in conversation compared
to smaller models (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct, Phi-4)</span>, with average
degradations of 30-40%. This is in part due to metric definitions. Since
smaller models achieve lower absolute scores in
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>, they have less
scope for degradation than the better models. In short, no matter how
strong an LLM’s single-turn performance is, we observe large performance
degradations in the multi-turn setting.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

When looking at the task-specific breakdown, some models see more muted
degradations in certain tasks. For instance, Command-A sees the least
degradation on the Actions task, while Claude 3.7 Sonnet and GPT-4.1
conserve performance well on Code, and Gemini 2.5 Pro in the
Data-to-Text task. This finding indicates that the multi-turn
capabilities of models are not uniform across domains and validates the
importance of benchmarking models across a wide variety of tasks to
investigate model capabilities.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Additional test-time compute (reasoning tokens) does not help models
navigate multi-turn underspecification, as the two reasoning models
included in the experiment (o3, Deepseek-R1) deteriorate in similar ways
to non-reasoning models. This result confirms that
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">additional test-time compute does
not, on its own, allow models to strategize over multi-turn
conversation</span>. The analysis we conduct identifies a potential root
cause: reasoning models tend to generate lengthier responses (on avg.
33% longer than non-reasoning LLMs). As we find in
Appendix <a href="#A6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F</span></a>, longer assistant responses
tend to contain more assumptions, which can derail the conversation by
confusing the model on what requirements were posed by the user vs. its
own previous turn responses.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.2 </span>Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis

<figure id="S6.F6" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S6.F6.sf1"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x5.png" id="S6.F6.sf1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_portrait" width="830"
height="1737" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(a) </span>Visualizing
Aptitude and Unreliability.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S6.F6.sf2"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x6.png" id="S6.F6.sf2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="829"
height="766" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(b) </span>Observed
Model Degradations</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S6.F6.sf3"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x7.png" id="S6.F6.sf3.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_portrait" width="830"
height="1640" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">(c) </span>Gradual
Sharding Results</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span>(<a
href="#S6.F6.sf1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(a)</span></a>) Visual introduction to the
concepts of Aptitude and Unreliability when overlaid on a box-plot
visualization, (<a href="#S6.F6.sf2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(b)</span></a>) reliability results based
on experimental simulations with 15 LLMs, (<a href="#S6.F6.sf3"
class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(c)</span></a>) summary of results from
gradual sharding experiment, with instructions sharded in gradually
larger shard sets (from 1 to 8 shards).</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S6.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Results presented in Table <a href="#S6.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> present averaged performance
degradation ($`\overline{P}`$). We now report on the aptitude and
reliability analysis based on metrics $`A`$ and $`U`$.
Figure <a href="#S6.F6.sf2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(b)</span></a> visually summarizes the
results of the reliability analysis we conducted on the 15 LLMs included
in our simulation experiment. First, looking at the two single-turn
settings, we see that models that are more able (higher A) tend to be
more reliable (lower U). For instance, the two most able models (GPT-4.1
and Gemini 2.5 Pro) achieve the lowest unreliability. At the lower end,
the two models with the lowest aptitude (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct and
OLMo-2-13B) are also the most unreliable. In summary,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">in single-turn settings, models
with higher aptitude tend to be more reliable.</span> This fact is known
in the community, with arguments made that better models require less
prompt engineering, as they are more robust to minor variations in
inputs and outputs \[<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The sharded setting paints a different picture. Model aptitude degrades
in a non-significant way between the full and sharded settings, with an
average drop of 16%. On the other hand, unreliability skyrockets with an
average increase of 112% (more than doubling). More interestingly,
though better models tend to have slightly higher multi-turn aptitude,
all models tend to have similar levels of unreliability. In other words,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">in multi-turn, underspecified
settings, all models we test exhibit very high unreliability, with
performance degrading 50 percent points on average between the best and
worst simulated run for a fixed instruction.</span> This refines our
definition of the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">lost in
conversation</span> phenomenon: when comparing single- and multi-turn
settings, we find that large performance degradations ($`\overline{P}`$)
are due in large part to increased model unreliability (U), rather than
a loss in aptitude (A).

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Appendix <a href="#A6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F</span></a> explores potential root causes
for models getting lost in conversations. We identify four specific
causes: (1) LLMs prematurely propose full answer attempts, making
assumptions about problem specifications that lead to confusion
(Appendix <a href="#A6.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="F.1 Premature Answer Attempts ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F.1</span></a>), (2) they overly rely on
previous (incorrect) answer attempts leading to lengthier “bloated”
answers (Section <a href="#A6.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="F.2 Answer Bloat in Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F.2</span></a>), (3) LLMs overly adjust
their answers based on the first and last turn of conversation,
evidenced by a loss-of-middle-turns phenomenon
(Appendix <a href="#A6.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="F.3 Over-adjust based on Last Turn of Conversation ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F.3</span></a>), and (4) they produce
overly verbose answers, which likely introduces assumptions that detract
attention from user utterances
(Section <a href="#A6.SS4" class="ltx_ref"
title="F.4 Overly-verbose Assistant Responses ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F.4</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.3 </span>Gradual Sharding Experiment

<div id="S6.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The multi-turn conversations simulated based on sharded conversations
are not representative of underspecified conversations that users might
have with LLMs in realistic settings. In particular, the fact that
sharded instructions must be maximal (property P3) and that the
simulated user must reveal at most one shard of information per turn
(Section <a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Simulating Sharded Conversations ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>) can seem unrealistic and
adversarial. In fact, prior work has found that minor and severe
underspecification appear in equal proportions in public LLM chat logs
\[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\]. To explore the
relationship between the granularity of sharding and the lost in
conversation phenomenon, we propose the gradual sharding experiment.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In the gradual sharding experiment, we selected 31 instructions from our
original experiment across multiple tasks, and expanded each sharded
instruction into seven sharded instructions, with the shard-set size
growing from 2 to 8 shards. The instruction selection and sharding
process are detailed in Appendix <a href="#A11" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix K Gradual Sharding Implementation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">K</span></a>. The process ensured that at
each shard set size (from 1 to 8), task complexity is fixed, and the
only modified factor is the granularity of sharding.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We ran simulations for the gradual sharding experiments with two models
(GPT-4o and GPT-4o-mini), with results summarized in
Figure <a href="#S6.F6.sf3" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Aptitude vs. Reliability Analysis ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6(c)</span></a>. We find that both models
get lost in conversation (a minor degradation in aptitude and a large
increase in unreliability) with two-shard instructions and beyond. In
other words, the gradual sharding experiment indicates that
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">any conversation that involves
underspecification and occurs in two or more turns leads to models
getting lost in conversation</span>. For users, the granularity at which
information is specified does not majorly impact reliability: providing
all the information at once (1-shard) is the only effective method to
improve reliability.

</div>

</div>

</div>

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7 </span>Implications

<div id="S7.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">7.1 </span>Implications for System and Agent Builders

</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 2:
</span>Experimental Results with additional simulation types: <span
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span
class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/recap.png"
id="S7.T2.m10.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="13"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span>  Recap and <span
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span
class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/snowball.png"
id="S7.T2.m11.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span>  Snowball. Both
strategies involve repeating user-turn information to mitigate models
getting lost in conversations.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S7.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Building LLM-based applications typically involves complex processes:
decomposition of problems, retrieval of relevant information, use of
tools, and calling of actions. Such processes are typically orchestrated
by an agentic framework (such as Autogen
\[<a href="#bib.bib84" class="ltx_ref">84</a>\] or LangChain
\[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>\]) that allows system
builders to compose workflows with LLM calls as individual blocks. As
such, an argument could be made that multi-turn capabilities are not a
necessary feature of LLMs, as it can be offloaded to the agent
framework. In other words, do we need native multi-turn support in LLMs
when an agent framework can orchestrate interactions with users and
leverage LLMs only as single-turn operators?

</div>

<div id="S7.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To answer this question, we implemented two agent-style conversation
simulation types:
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/recap.png" id="S7.SS1.p2.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="13" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span>
</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> and
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/snowball.png"
id="S7.SS1.p2.m2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span>
</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span>. Both
preprocess user utterances before sending them to the LLM. In
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span>, a conversation
proceeds in the same way as
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>, but a user
turn is added at the end, which recapitulates all the previous user
turns. <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span> is a
more gradual recapitulation: at each turn, the user simulator reveals a
new shard, and repeats all previously revealed shards at that point.
Both simulation types repeat the past user’s turn information to make it
more prominent and give the LLM a chance to leverage the redundancy to
improve its responses. We include the experimental detail in
Appendix <a href="#A13" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix M Recap &amp; Snowball Experiment Implementation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">M</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Table <a href="#S7.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 7.1 Implications for System and Agent Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> summarizes the results on all
instructions for four tasks (Code, Database, Math, Actions) for two
tested models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini). Both
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span> demonstrate
some level of success, with improvements over
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulations,
but the performance still lags behind
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> or
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>. While
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> outperforms
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span>, we note that
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> is an unrealistic
setting because the intervention is conducted on the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">last turn</span> of the
conversation, which is not known a priori when conversation unfolds with
a real user. <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span>
gives a sense of realistic performance gains achievable through
user-turn repetition: it can mitigate the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>-to-<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
performance deterioration by 15-20%. In short, relying on an agent-like
framework to process information might be limiting, and we argue LLMs
should natively support multi-turn interaction.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">7.2 </span>Implications for LLM Builders

<div id="S7.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A lot of effort has been put in improving LLM
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">aptitude</span>: demonstrating
that LLMs can accomplish tasks of increasing intellectual complexity,
with recent results showing LLMs can compete in mathematics Olympiads,
or solve Ph.D.-level technical questions in a benchmark aptly named
Humanity’s Last Exam \[<a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S7.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In this work, we call on LLM builders to prioritize
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">reliability</span> of the models
they build, as our experiments demonstrate that the randomness involved
in generating text with LLMs leads to catastrophic unreliability in all
the models we tested, degrading the quality of responses the average LLM
users see.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

LLMs are probabilistic systems, with parameters such as
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">temperature</span> that can
adjust the degree of randomness that occurs while generating text. A
possible argument is therefore: does setting the temperature to its
lowest setting ($`T=0`$) effectively resolve the reliability concern, as
it makes the generation process more (but not entirely) deterministic?

</div>

<div id="S7.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To evaluate this argument, we conducted a supplementary experiment in
which the assistant’s temperature for generating responses (AT) was
varied to three values: 1.0, 0.5, and 0.0. Additionally, since
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulation uses
an LLM-based user simulator, we also varied the user’s temperature (UT)
with the same three values. Further details on the experiment, including
sample selection and simulation scale, are in
Appendix <a href="#A12" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix L Temperature Experiment Implementation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">L</span></a>.

</div>
