<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3:
</span>Unreliability of models when changing assistant temperature (AT)
and user temperature (UT) in <span
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span
class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/full.png"
id="S7.T3.m11.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>, <span
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span
class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/concat.png"
id="S7.T3.m12.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="15"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> and <span
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span
class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/sharded.png"
id="S7.T3.m13.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="16"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> settings. The lower
the number the more reliable the assistant is.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S7.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Table <a href="#S7.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ 7.2 Implications for LLM Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> summarizes the experimental
findings. Looking at the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> settings (first
two rows), both GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4o observe a large improvement in
reliability when temperature is decreased, with a drop in unreliability
($`U_{10}^{90}`$) of 50-80% when the assistant temperature decreases.
Results from <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
simulations are more alarming: GPT-4o-mini does not see improvements in
reliability as AT is decreased (in all user-temperature settings), and
GPT-4o only sees minor improvements, on the order of 15-20%. Even when
both the user and assistant temperatures are set to 0.0, there remains a
large unreliability of around 30%. Even though language models are
supposed to be deterministic at $`T=0.0`$, this is known to practically
not be the case for modern LLMs (see
Appendix <a href="#A14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix N On obtaining deterministic outputs from LLMs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">N</span></a> for discussion). At a high
level, single-turn conversations have limited scope for deviation,
whereas one token difference in an early turn of a multi-turn
conversation can lead to cascading deviations, which we observe as
stagnated unreliability. For settings that involve multi-turn
interaction, we find that <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">lowering
the temperature of the LLM when generating responses is ineffective in
improving system reliability.</span>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We invite and challenge LLM builders to jointly optimize model aptitude
and reliability. A reliable LLM should: (1) achieve similar aptitude in
single- and multi-turn settings, (2) have small unreliability
($`U_{10}^{90}<15`$) in multi-turn settings, (3) achieve these at
unmodified temperature ($`T=1.0`$), demonstrating that the underlying
language model can handle variations that naturally occur in language
generation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">7.3 </span>Implications for NLP Practitioners

<div id="S7.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Our experiments demonstrate that model behavior in single- and
multi-turn settings on the same underlying set of instructions can
diverge in important ways, for example, with large observed degradations
in performance and reliability.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We selected the initial six tasks to span a wide range of generation
tasks, from programming to multi-document summarization. Yet this set of
tasks is limited across multiple dimensions, such as focusing on
English-language instructions and analytical (i.e., non-creative) tasks.
We put effort into making the sharding process scalable by automating
portions that could be handled by an LLM, while manually validating and
finalizing samples for quality control. The sharding process – detailed
in Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a> – required an average of three
hours of manual work (prompt engineering or inspection) from an author
to prepare and finalize 100 sharded instructions.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We encourage NLP practitioners to experiment with sharding and release
sharded versions of their tasks and instructions alongside fully
specified ones.

</div>

height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> translation task
for <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/full.png"
id="S7.T4.m12.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>, <span

id="S7.T4.m13.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="15"

class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>, and <span

class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/sharded.png"
id="S7.T4.m14.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="16"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
simulations.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S7.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To illustrate the feasibility of sharding new tasks, and understand
compatibility requirements for sharding, we prepared sharded
instructions for a seventh task:
<span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom">
<span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/translation.png"
id="S7.SS3.p4.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Translation. The
task consists of translating an entire document (10 sentences) from
German to English, leveraging paired documents from WMT 2019 on
document-level translation
\[<a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>\]. In the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting, each
turn reveals two additional sentences from the source document and
requires the assistant to translate all sentences provided so far,
whereas the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> settings reveal
the entire document in the first turn. Evaluation is conducted with the
standard BLEU metric \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\]. We
describe practical implementation details in
Appendix <a href="#A9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix I Task-specific Implementation details ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">I</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS3.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Results from <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>, and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulations are
summarized in Table <a href="#S7.T4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 4 ‣ 7.3 Implications for NLP Practitioners ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>. Both models we tested –
GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4o – do
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">not</span> exhibit degradation in
performance in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting, with
BLEU scores being within 10% difference of each other in all settings.
We believe this result reflects that the task can largely be
accomplished at the sentence-level despite some prior work has framed
translation at the document-level
\[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\], and that the BLEU score
does not adequately capture document-level nuances
\[<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>\]. In other words, if a
task is episodic (i.e., it can be decomposed into turn-level subtasks),
the models can avoid getting lost in conversation by completing each
subtask without having to handle multi-turn context. In short, the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> Translation
task simulates multi-turn conversations that are not underspecified.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS3.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We now list task properties we believe are important in leading models
to get lost in conversation in multi-turn settings. First, generative
tasks (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, unlike
extractive QA or classification) are more prone to model confusion, as
they typically involve editing and refinement of new content. Second,
the generative tasks should be sufficiently complex, involving multiple
explicit specifications that will yield a multitude of shards. For
example, an instruction: “Write a Python program that calculates
$`1+1`$” is too simple to shard. Third, the solution or answer should be
non-decomposable, such that revealing a shard modifies the entire
solution (unlike the translation task, where each additional shard only
asks to translate and append to the ongoing solution). We hypothesize
that LLMs tested on tasks with the aforementioned three properties will
likely get lost in conversation, evidenced by a large drop in averaged
performance and reliability in
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">7.4 </span>Implications for Users of Conversational Systems

<div id="S7.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Users of LLM-based products should be aware of the lack of reliability
of LLMs, particularly when used in multi-turn settings. Generally
available generative technology is new, and prior work has identified
the randomness in LLM-generated text as a point of confusion for users
\[<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">81</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>\]. We make two practical
recommendations that can help users of LLM-based systems get the most
out of their exchanges.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS4.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### If time allows, try again.

<div id="S7.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

If a conversation with an LLM did not lead to expected outcomes,
starting a new conversation that repeats the same information might
yield significantly better outcomes than continuing an ongoing
conversation. This is because current LLMs can get lost in the
conversation, and our experiments show that persisting in a conversation
with the model is ineffective. In addition, since LLMs generate text
with randomness, a new conversation may lead to improved outcomes.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS4.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Consolidate before retrying.

<div id="S7.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Since LLMs are ineffective at dealing with information dispersed across
multiple turns, consolidating instruction requirements into a single
instruction is an effective strategy to improve the model’s aptitude and
reliability (as shown by the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> experiments).
When a user notices that a model is lost in conversation, they can ask
the LLM: “Please consolidate everything I’ve told you so far,” then
bring the response to a new conversation, alleviating the need for
manual consolidation. In practice, there is anecdotal evidence that
early adopters of LLM-based applications are aware that LLMs get lost in
conversation. For example, users of the Cursor LLM-based coding
environment report that frequently creating new conversations “whenever
they can” is a recommended strategy to ensure high quality responses
even though the tool allows to keep conversations going
indefinitely.<span id="footnote2"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span><a
href="https://www.reddit.com/r/cursor/comments/1j72r8d/when_to_start_a_new_chat/"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://www.reddit.com/r/cursor/comments/1j72r8d/when_to_start_a_new_chat/</a></span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

These two recommendations remain cumbersome for users and can only offer
patched solutions rather than a principled approach. Once future LLMs
can more reliably handle multi-turn conversations, the need for such
recommendations should be alleviated, allowing users to communicate
underspecified instructions over multiple turns naturally with less risk
of the model getting lost in conversation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S8" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">8 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S8.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In this work, we conduct a large-scale simulation of single- and
multi-turn conversations with LLMs, and find that on a fixed set of
tasks, LLM performance degrades significantly in multi-turn,
underspecified settings. LLMs get lost in conversation, which
materializes as a significant decrease in reliability as models struggle
to maintain context across turns, make premature assumptions, and
over-rely on their previous responses. Additional experiments reveal
that known remediations that work for simpler settings (such as
agent-like concatenation or decreasing temperature during generation)
are ineffective in multi-turn settings, and we call on LLM builders to
prioritize the reliability of models in multi-turn settings.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S9" class="section ltx_section">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">9 </span>Limitations

<div id="S9.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A first limitation of our work is the reliance on fully automated
simulation. By relying on an LLM to simulate user utterances, we can
scale our experiments, including running the same simulation multiple
times, which would be cost-prohibitive with real users. However, the
simulations we obtain are not representative of natural human-AI
conversation. The properties of the sharding process (defined in
Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>) and of the simulation
environment (see Section <a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Simulating Sharded Conversations ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>) ensure that the simulated
conversations follow a rather narrow structure, likely not modeling the
full range of conversation dynamics that occur with a large, diverse
user population. For example, the simulation process ensures a new shard
of information is revealed at each turn, and that the last turn of the
conversation has specified all the information needed to complete the
task which might not happen with real users. Properties P1, P2, and P5
of the sharding process also restrict the scope of the conversation, as
sharded instructions closely match an existing fully-specified
instruction, with the high-level intent always identified in the
conversation’s first turn. The minimal nature of shards is also
unrealistic and potentially adversarial, though the gradual sharding
experiment finds that different levels of shard granularity lead to
similar performance degradations, as soon as conversations occur over
two turns or more. Apart from sharding granularity, automatic simulation
also lacks the nuance that can occur when a human is involved in
conversation, from misunderstandings over terminology, giving up due to
frustration with system failures
\[<a href="#bib.bib82" class="ltx_ref">82</a>\], or the lack of a
feasible end goal for certain conversations (e.g., the user wanting a
solution to an unsolved problem). Because of these factors, we believe
conducted simulations represent a benign testing ground for LLM
multi-turn capabilities. <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Because of
the overly simplified conditions of simulation, we believe the
degradation observed in experiments is most likely an underestimate of
LLM unreliability, and how frequently LLMs get lost in conversation in
real-world settings.</span> The experiments serve as a scalable,
low-cost experimental environment for studying LLMs in multi-turn
settings.

</div>

<div id="S9.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A second limitation of our work is the focus on analytical tasks.
Although we selected a diverse set of both programming and natural
language tasks, we restricted experiments to tasks that involve an
analytical solution. This restriction limits the scope of our findings,
as we do not establish whether models get lost in conversation on more
open-ended tasks, such as creative writing
\[<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>\]. This was a conscious
choice: though there has been some progress on creative writing
evaluation, it is still an active area of research
\[<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>\], and we relied on more
established tasks and metrics for the initial set of experiments.
Determining whether degradation occurs – and if so, identifying the
magnitude – on creative tasks is an important direction for future work.

</div>

<div id="S9.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A third limitation of the work is the focus on text-only tasks in the
English language. Establishing whether models get lost in conversation
in other languages, or in tasks that involve multiple modalities in
either user or assistant utterances, could help establish the scope of
the degradation observed in LLM multi-turn capabilities.

</div>

<div id="S9.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  

</div>

</div>

<div id="Ptx1" class="section ltx_part">

## Appendices

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Related work on Underspecification

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Background (Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Background and Related Work ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) reviews the most directly
related prior work, focused on multi-turn evaluation. We now cover other
related prior works that have studied underspecification.

</div>

<div id="A1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Prior work on communication and linguistics has identified
underspecification as a common feature of human language
\[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">61</a>\].

</div>

<div id="A1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Understanding how LLMs handle underspecified instructions is crucial
towards improving conversational capabilities. To this end, Herlihy
et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\] identified common
response patterns such as hedging, refusal, clarification, and
interrogation when underspecified queries are presented to
conversational LLM systems, and proposed mechanisms to recover from
them. Malaviya et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>\]
highlighted the importance of supporting context for more accurate and
principled evaluation of LLM responses on underspecified queries, and
Sarkar et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref">69</a>\] showed
that a system that proactively rewrites user instructions to account for
underspecification leads to improved LLM response. Shaikh et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib71" class="ltx_ref">71</a>\] studied the degree of
grounding (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>,
clarifications and follow-up questions) that LLMs perform in
conversation logs and observed that they significantly lack in
generating follow-up questions, where humans are 15 times more likely to
do so. Chang et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>\] hired
annotators to manually reproduce fully-specified instructions through a
chat interface, and found that the users reveal the entirety of the
instruction in 34% of the time, leaving some detail underspecified a
majority of the time.

</div>

<div id="A1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Several works have explored direct tasks to evaluate model ability when
dealing with underspecification. Liu et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">49</a>\] introduced AmbiEnt, a
natural language inference benchmark, which revealed that understanding
ambiguous statements is still a challenge even to the state-of-the-art
LLMs. Wildenburg et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib83" class="ltx_ref">83</a>\]
created the DUST task, which requires the language model to determine
the relative levels of specifications between two sentences, finding
that when interpreting underspecified sentences, LMs exhibit little
uncertainty. Vijayvargiya et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">78</a>\] evaluated LLM agents for
GitHub issue resolution in an underspecified setting, showing that
follow-up interactions to supplement information helps improve the
resolve rate but detecting the ambiguities in the instructions remains a
challenge.

</div>

<div id="A1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Prior work has classified different root causes for underspecification.
First, task underspecification occurs when humans provide incomplete
descriptions of the task at hand, which is prominent in
“specification-heavy tasks”
\[<a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref">60</a>\]. Second, intent
misalignment occur when the AI fails to understand the user’s intent or
motivation, and is one of the common sources of user dissatisfaction
\[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a>\]. Finally, Chaturvedi
et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>\] discuss location
and and reference ambiguity, in emboddied settings that involve physical
spaces such as a Minecraft game.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Precise Definition of Sharded Instructions

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Section <a href="#S3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.1 Sharding Process: From Fully-Specified to Sharded Instructions ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a> introduces the concept of
sharding at a high level. This Appendix offers a more precise definition
by first defining mathematical terminology, and then defining properties
that a sharded instruction must satisfy to be considered valid.

</div>

<div id="A2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Let $`q`$ refer to a single-turn complex query with intended (i.e.,
correct) output $`Y_{q}^{*}`$. We refer to the atomic content units
(ACU) \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>\] of the query as

|     |                                           |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                           
       ``` math                                   
       I(q)=[\mathcal{I},({c}_{1},\cdots,c_{m})]  
       ```                                        |     |

where $`\mathcal{I}`$ is the primary intent of the query and
$`(c_{1},\cdots,c_{m})`$ are the sufficient set of clarifications that
specify details of how to compute $`Y_{q}^{*}`$ conditioned on
$`\mathcal{I}`$. For $`I(q)`$ to be considered
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">atomic</span>, any rephrasing of
$`I(q)`$ should produce the same target output. Ie. for all
$`q^{\prime}`$ s.t. $`I(q^{\prime})=I(q)`$, then
$`Y_{q}^{\prime*}=Y_{q}^{*}`$.

</div>

<div id="A2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Given the above definition, the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">aim</span> of the sharding
process, for a given query $`q`$, is to identify the atomic content
units $`I(q)`$ and construct a set of shorter instruction
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">shards</span> $`\mathbf{s}`$:

|     |                                                                  |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |                                                                  
       ``` math                                                          
       q^{\prime}=[s_{1},\cdots s_{k}]\;\mbox{ s.t. }I(q)=I(q^{\prime})  
       ```                                                               |     |

where the shards $`s_{j}`$ can be used to simulate multi-turn
conversation, with the same intended output as $`q`$.

</div>

<div id="A2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A sharded instruction $`q^{\prime}`$ is considered valid for an original
query $`q`$ if it fulfills the following properties:

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### P1: Information Preservation.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

$`I(q)=I(q^{\prime})`$ No information from the original instruction
necessary for the completion of the instruction should be lost during
the sharding process.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### P2: Clear Initial Intent.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

$`\mathcal{I}_{q}=\mathcal{I}_{q^{\prime}}`$ and
$`s_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{q}`$. The first shard plays a distinctive role of
being the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">initial query</span>
within the shard set. The initial query defines the high-level objective
for the entire conversation. (e.g., “write a Python function”).

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### P3: Order Insensitive.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Apart from the first shard, the other shards should be decontextualized
\[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\] and not refer to each
other in a way that implies an order. As a result, the shard set
presented in any order reveals equivalent information. Let
$`\rho(\mathbf{s}_{2..k})`$ refer to a permutation of the shard
ordering, then
$`I(q)=I(\tilde{q})\;\forall\tilde{q}=[s_{1},\rho(\mathbf{s}_{2..k})]`$

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### P4: Maximal Sharding.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The sharding process should strive to maximize the number of shards
extracted from the original instruction (maximize $`k`$). This can be
achieved by producing shards that introduce a single, specific piece of
information.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### P5: Minimal Transformation.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The sharded instruction should maintain the instruction language and
avoid simplifying, altering, or interpreting elements of the original
instruction as much as possible. Apart from modifications to satisfy
properties P1-P4, the sharding process should attempt to limit
modifications such that the shards ($`[s_{1},\cdots s_{k}]`$ are
semantically similar to the atomic content units $`I(q)`$.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Semi-Automatic Sharding Process

<figure id="A3.F7" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x8.png" id="A3.F7.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="276" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 7:
</span>Process diagram of the four-step semi-automatic process to
transform fully-specified instructions into a sharded instruction. The
first three steps (segmentation, rephrasing, verification) are
automated, while the fourth (inspect and edit) was manually completed by
the authors of the work. The last row represents the rejection criteria
for a sample.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We rely on a semi-automatic process to transform fully-specified
instructions into their sharded equivalents. The process – illustrated
in Figure <a href="#A3.F7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 7 ‣ Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> – consists of a sequence of
three automated steps (Segmentation, Rephrasing, Verification) followed
by a manual step that was conducted by an author of the paper.

</div>

<div id="A3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We now detail each step of the process, then go over task-specific
details we implemented as needed. We note that as part of our
open-source release, we provide all the prompts used in the first three
LLM-based steps.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Step 1: Segmentation

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Given an original fully-specified instruction (left-most column in
Figure <a href="#A3.F7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 7 ‣ Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>), the LLM is prompted to
extract <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">segments</span> of the
instructions. Segments are intended to correspond to the atomic content
units (defined in Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix B Precise Definition of Sharded Instructions ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>). In particular, the prompt
indicates that segments must not overlap, and that not all words in the
original instruction must belong to a segment. Prompts are task-specific
and incorporate at least three few-shot examples of segmentation, to
allow for the concept of segmentation to be illustrated through
examples. At this stage, any instruction that yields fewer than three
segments are filtered out and does not proceed to the next stage.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Step 2: Rephrasing

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Given the original fully-specified instruction and the extracted
segments, this stage consists in rewriting each segment to be
decontextualized \[<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">13</a>\] and
conversational. In other words, dependencies between segments are
resolved, and the ordering is changed such that obtained
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">shards</span> adhere to
properties P2 and P5. In the example above, the fourth segment
(highlighted in orange) becomes the first shard as it reveals the
overall intent, and light rephrasing occur in other shards. The
rephrasing prompt is task-specific, and includes few-shot examples of
rephrasing segmented instructions.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Step 3: Verification

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Steps 1-2 produce a sharded instruction that can be used to simulate
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> conversations.
To verify the property P1 (Information Preservation) that no information
has been lost during segmentation and rephrasing, we conduct preliminary
simulations to evaluate the original and sharded instruction
side-by-side. Specifically for each pair of the original and the sharded
instruction, we simulate ten
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> conversations with
the original instruction, ten
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> conversations
with the sharded instruction (by concatenating the shards), and ten
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Shuffle-concat</span>
conversations.
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Shuffle-concat</span> is a
variant of the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>
simulation in which all shards (except Shard 1) are randomly permuted
before being concatenated. This variant can be seen as a more
adversarial version of
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>, verifying the
property P3 (Order Insensitive). For each simulation type, we calculate
the averaged performance $`\overline{P}`$ over ten runs and filter out
instructions that are below an acceptable degradation threshold.
Specifically, instructions are acceptable if the following conditions
are met:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{P}_{\textsc{Concat}}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 0.8~\overline{P}_{\textsc{Full}}`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{P}_{\textsc{Shuffle-concat}}`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq 0.8~\overline{P}_{\textsc{Full}},`$ |  |

where $`\overline{P}_{\textsc{X}}`$ denotes the averaged performance of
the simulation type <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">X</span>.
If more degradation is observed
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, below 80%), it
indicates a potential loss of information during sharding, or that
decontextualization was not implemented accurately.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Step 4: Inspect and Edit

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Even though the first three steps define the sharding process and
implement some level of quality assurance, they do not guarantee the
level of quality required for precise and large-scale experiments due to
relying on LLM outputs. To obtain high-quality shards, we reserve step 4
for manual inspection and validation. To facilitate the procedure, we
developed a web-based annotation interface. In the interface, an
annotator can review a pair of fully-specified and sharded instructions,
edit, add, or remove individual shards, and decide to accept or reject
sharded instructions. Sharded instructions included in our experiments
were all manually reviewed by two authors of the work. The amount of
editing and filtering required in this final stage varied by task.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Inspecting and editing an auto-generated instruction typically requires
1-3 minutes per instruction, an order of magnitude less than it would
require for authors to write the sharded instructions de-novo from a
given fully-specified instruction. As part of our open-source release,
we provide all the prompts used during sharding, which we hope can
facilitate the sharding of additional tasks.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 5: </span>Results
of the inspection of 100 simulated sharded conversations across four
tasks: Actions, Code, Math, and Database. The first column aggregates
annotation results on the four tasks.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The sharding simulation environment (described in
Section <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>) relies on LLM components to
simulate the user, classify assistant responses, and extract answers
from free-text responses. LLM-based components are likely to fail, and
we performed an inspection of 200 simulated
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> conversations
to understand the level of simulation error and the potential effect on
estimating the performance of the assistant LLMs due to the error.

</div>

<div id="A4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

For each inspected conversation, we annotated user turns, assistant
turns, and the overall conversation with five specific elements.

</div>

<div id="A4.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

For user utterances, we annotated whether the utterance revealed exactly
the information from one shard in the sharded instruction
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Shard Fully
Revealed</span>). Specifically, we flagged turns that revealed more than
one shard, and turns that revealed a shard only partially. We also
annotated each user’s turn for whether it is appropriately
contextualized in the conversation
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Shard
Contextualized</span>). For example, if the previous assistant’s turn
asked a binary clarification question (yes/no), then proper
contextualization would require a Yes/No response to directly address
the assistant’s response.

</div>

<div id="A4.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

For assistant utterances, we annotated whether the classified strategy
was accurate (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Strategy
Accuracy</span>). For example, if the response is labeled as a
clarification, we confirm if it poses a clarification question to the
user. When assistant utterances were labeled as answer attempts, we
further labeled whether the answer extraction step was successful
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Extraction Success</span>).

</div>

<div id="A4.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Upon completing the inspection of each user and assistance utterance, we
assigned a global label to the entire conversation on whether or not the
errors that occurred during simulation (if any) affected the overall
validity of the simulation. If not, the simulation was marked as
successful (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Overall
Success</span>).

</div>

<div id="A4.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We inspected conversations for four tasks: Actions, Code, Math and
Database. The other two (Summary and Data-to-text) are refining tasks
that require an answer attempt at each turn, and do not rely on an
LLM-based user simulator. As such, they have limited scope for
simulation error.

</div>

<div id="A4.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Table <a href="#A4.T5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 5 ‣ Appendix D Inspection of Simulated Sharded Conversation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> summarizes the results of the
inspection annotation. Overall, the simulation environment is highly
reliable, with roughly 98% of inspected conversations labeled as
successful. Some errors occur in each component. With user simulation, a
single shard is fully revealed around 96% of the time, and properly
contextualized 98% of the time. The processing of assistant responses
also leads to errors: the turn strategy classification is only 95%
accurate, and extraction of answer attempts has an accuracy of 97%.

</div>

<div id="A4.p8" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Utterance-level errors did not always affect the validity of the overall
simulation. In some cases, we observed that the user simulator would
correct an error in an early turn, subsequently in the conversation, or
that an error in answer extraction on the wrong answer attempt would
occur at a turn, but the extraction would be successful later on. In
summary, we empirically find that the simulation environment is largely
accurate: though some errors occur, large drops of performance in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting (beyond
2%) are not due to errors caused by the simulator.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix E </span>Concrete Example of Loss in Aptitude vs. Reliability

<div id="A5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Let’s imagine we are provided with ten instructions ($`N=10`$), each
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>. We run
simulations with an LLM, simulating 10 conversations per instruction and
setting ($`M=10`$). Let’s assume the LLM achieves an averaged
performance ($`\overline{P}`$) of 90% in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>, and 60% in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting.

</div>

<div id="A5.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Finally, let’s assume that the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> performance is
achieved by having perfect performance
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, success in 10/10
randomly sampled runs) on 9 instructions, and failing on all the sampled
simulations of the last, tenth instruction. In other words:

</div>

<div id="A5.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
S^{\textsc{Full}}_{ij}=\begin{cases}100,&\text{if }i\in\{1,\dots,9\}\\
0,&\text{if }i=10\end{cases},
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A5.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

where $`S^{\textsc{Full}}_{ij}`$ represents the score for $`i`$-th
instruction at $`j`$-th simulation run. The aptitude ($`A`$) and
unreliability ($`U`$) of the LLM for the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> setting is
$`A=90`$% and $`U=0`$%
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, for each
instruction, the 10th and 90th percentile scores are equal).

</div>

<figure id="A5.F8" class="ltx_figure ltx_align_floatright">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x9.png" id="A5.F8.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="830"
height="830" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 8:
</span>Illustrations for different situations. Green and red fills in
each grid indicate sample-level score (<span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.,</span> pass / exact match).
Compared to <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> (top
left), three situations in <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> achieve the same
<span class="math inline">$\overline{P}=60$</span> while varying in
aptitude <span class="math inline"><em>A</em></span> and unreliability
<span class="math inline"><em>U</em></span>.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A5.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Let’s now consider three conditions for the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting that
all achieve an averaged performance of $`\overline{P}=60`$%. We
illustrate the conditions in Figure <a href="#A5.F8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 8 ‣ Appendix E Concrete Example of Loss in Aptitude vs. Reliability ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Situation 1: Drop in Aptitude.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The LLM achieves perfect performance on six of the ten instructions:

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
S^{\textsc{Sharded}}_{ij}=\begin{cases}100,&\text{if }i\in\{1,\dots,6\}\\
0,&\text{if }i\in\{7,\dots,10\}\end{cases}.
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In situation 1, $`\overline{P}=60`$%, $`A=60`$%, and $`U=0`$%. The
degradation in performance is entirely explained by a decrease in
aptitude, while the reliability remains the same.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Situation 2: Drop in Reliability.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The LLM achieves mixed performance (6-7 perfect scores per instruction)
on nine of the 10 instructions:

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
S^{\textsc{Sharded}}_{ij}=\begin{cases}100,&\text{if }1\leq i\leq 3,1\leq j\leq 6\\
100,&\text{if }4\leq i\leq 9,1\leq j\leq 7\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In situation 2, $`\overline{P}=60`$%, with an aptitude of $`A=90`$%, and
a unreliability of $`U=90`$%. The degradation in performance is entirely
explained by a large drop in reliability, while sharded and
fully-specified aptitude are equal.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Situations 1 and 2 illustrate extreme scenarios where the average drop
in performance is entirely explained by a drop in aptitude or
reliability, but in practice a combination is more likely to occur, as
in situation 3.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Situation 3: Combined drop in Aptitude and Reliability.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The LLM achieves perfect performance on three instructions, and mixed
performance (6 perfect scores per instruction) on five of the 10
instructions:

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
S^{\textsc{Sharded}}_{ij}=\begin{cases}100,&\text{if }1\leq i\leq 3\\
100,&\text{if }4\leq i\leq 8,1\leq j\leq 6\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.
``` |  |

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In situation 3, $`\overline{P}=60`$%, with an aptitude of $`A=80`$%, and
a unreliability of $`U=60`$%. Note that situation 3 leads to a larger
increase in unreliability (from 0% to 60%) than a decrease in aptitude
(from 90% to 80%) when compared to fully-specific simulations. This
corresponds in practice to our observation: drops in performance are
explained by small drops in aptitude and large drops in reliability.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Finally, we note that though this concrete example we provide uses
binary scores (0 and 100) for simulated conversation outcomes, aptitude
(A) and unreliability (U) can equally be applied to continuous metrics
(such as BLEU).

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A6" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix F </span>Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs

<div id="A6.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In the following subsections, we report qualitative analyses on the
corpus of simulations from the main experiment
(Section <a href="#S6.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="6.1 Average Performance Findings ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.1</span></a>). The purpose of the
analyses is to discern root causes in model behavior that lead to
performance degradation. We identify four behaviors below and provide
the analysis for each item in the rest of the section:

</div>

<div id="A6.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

1.  <span id="A6.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">1.</span></span>
    <div id="A6.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

    LLMs attempt to answer the entire problem prematurely.

    </div>
2.  <span id="A6.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">2.</span></span>
    <div id="A6.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

    LLMs overly rely on previous (incorrect) answer attempts, leading to
    lengthier “bloated” answers.

    </div>
3.  <span id="A6.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">3.</span></span>
    <div id="A6.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

    LLMs overly adjust their answers based on the last conversation
    turn, materialized by a pronounced forgetting of middle-turns.

    </div>
4.  <span id="A6.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">4.</span></span>
    <div id="A6.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

    LLMs produce answers that are overly verbose, which likely introduce
    problem assumptions that detract attention from user-utterances.

    </div>

</div>

<div id="A6.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">F.1 </span>Premature Answer Attempts

<figure id="A6.T6" class="ltx_table ltx_align_floatleft">
<div class="ltx_inline-block ltx_transformed_outer"
style="width:433.6pt;height:565.6pt;vertical-align:-279.5pt;">
<span class="ltx_transformed_inner"
style="transform:translate(75.3pt,-49.7pt) scale(1.53198386101797,1.53198386101797) ;">
</span>
<table class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<tbody>

At First Answer Attempt</td>

</tr>
<tr class="ltx_tr">
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_justify ltx_align_top ltx_border_t"

style="background-color:#F5F5F5;"><span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic"

…</span></span></span> </span></td>
<td class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t"

class="ltx_text" style="background-color:#F5F5F5;"><span

</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6: </span>Averaged
performance (<span class="math inline">$\overline{P}$</span>) breakdown,
based on how early in the conversation the LLM makes its first answer
attempt. Analysis conducted on simulations of two tasks: Code and
Math.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A6.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

During <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
simulation, responses are classified according to a seven-class
conversation strategy categorization. In particular, each assistant
response is tagged as being a formal
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">answer attempt</span> or not (as
answer attempts require further processing: extraction and evaluation by
the task-specific evaluator).

</div>

<div id="A6.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

On the onset of conversation, LLMs have the least amount of information
(highest level of underspecification), and are least likely to formulate
correct answer attempts. Proposing a solution early might therefore
plant certain incorrect elements in it, which wrongly influences the
interaction later in the conversation.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To evaluate this hypothesis, we bin all simulated conversations from our
experiments based on how early in the conversation the first answer
attempt is generated by the LLM. Specifically, we create five bins:
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">0-20%</span> if the first
answer attempt occurs within the first 20% turns of the conversation,
and <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">20-40%</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">40-60%</span>,
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">60-80%</span>, and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">80-100%</span> if it occurs
in later turns of the conversation. Of the six tasks included in our
experiments, only two (Math and Code) observed a significant range in
LLM behavior for answer attempt timing. For the other four tasks, models
attempt an answer from the first turn in most of the time, rendering
analysis on this parameter impossible.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Analysis results for the two remaining tasks are presented in
Table <a href="#A6.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 6 ‣ F.1 Premature Answer Attempts ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>. We observe that for every
single model, conversations with a later first answer attempt lead to
higher averaged performance. Across all models, conversations with the
first attempt being made in the first 20% of conversations achieve a
score of 30.9, less than half of the 64.4 when the LLM waits for the
last 20% of the conversation to make an answer attempt.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In other words, we find that premature answer attempts detract LLM
performance. Conversations where the model clarifies user instructions
or discusses the problem at a high-level before moving to generating
complete answer attempts lead to higher performance. We hypothesize that
this is due to the model making incorrect assumptions in premature
solutions, which conflict with subsequent user instructions in later
turns.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A6.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">F.2 </span>Answer Bloat in Multi-Turn Conversation

<figure id="A6.F9" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x10.png" id="A6.F9.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="813"
height="203" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 9:
</span>Average length (in number of characters) of answer attempts
across four tasks (Code, Database, Data-to-text, and Summary) in <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> conversations. Answer
attempts in the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>
and <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> settings
tend to be shorter on average than those from <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting. <span
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> answer attempts
increase in length as the LLMs make more answer attempts.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A6.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In multi-turn conversation simulations, the LLM might make multiple
answer attempts, with each subsequent attempt being potentially based on
previous attempts. In contrast, single-turn conversations constrain
conversation dynamics, with the LLM making a single, first-and-final
answer attempt.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To understand multi-turn conversation dynamics, we calculate the average
length of answer attempts in each simulation type. For the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting, we
calculate average length for each attempt within a simulation
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, average length of
the first attempt, second attempt, third attempt, etc.). We note for
readers here that the analysis is conducted on extracted answer attempts
(output of the Answer Extractor module in
Figure <a href="#S3.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 3 ‣ 3.2 Simulating Sharded Conversations ‣ 3 Simulating Underspecified, Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>) rather than the entire
assistant responses. The extracted answer more accurately measures
dynamics in answer attempts (i.e., generated SQL query, or Python
function) rather than the entire responses, which might contain varying
amounts of unrelated content.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Results of the analysis are plotted in
Figure <a href="#A6.F9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 9 ‣ F.2 Answer Bloat in Multi-Turn Conversation ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a>. Across the four tasks, we
find that answer lengths in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> settings tend to
be similar, typically within 2-10% of each other. On three of the
analyzed tasks (Code, Database, Summary), the first answer attempt in
the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting has
a similar length to
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> counterparts,
yet for each subsequent answer attempt, we observe an increase in
average answer length. The effect is such that the final answer attempts
in <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
conversations (right portion of the four plots) tend to be 20-300%
longer than the solutions generated in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> settings. We
name this observation the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">answer
bloat effect</span>: as a multi-turn conversation progresses, the LLM
generates incorrect answer attempts, making assumptions about portions
of the instruction that remain unspecified. As the user reveals
additional information in succeeding turns, the LLM does not
successfully invalidate its prior assumptions and overly relies on its
previous attempts. Answer bloat in multi-turn, underspecified
conversation leads to longer solutions compared to single-turn
equivalents.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We perform an additional analysis, focusing only on the Code and
Database tasks and filtering to simulations where the LLM reaches an
entirely correct solution (score of 100.0). For Code task, correct
programs obtained from
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting are on
average 850 characters long, which is 27% more characters than the
correct solutions generated in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> setting (668
characters on average). For Database, correct SQL queries in the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> setting are on
average 129 characters, 14% more characters than those from the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> setting (113
characters). In summary, LLMs are less likely to reach a correct
solution in multi-turn settings (lower $`\overline{P}`$), and when they
do, the final solutions they reach are longer (bloated), hinting that
the solutions are qualitatively worse.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A6.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">F.3 </span>Over-adjust based on Last Turn of Conversation

<div id="A6.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Because the summary task requires the assistant to attribute its summary
back to documents through citation, the task offers a unique opportunity
to analyze what turns of information LLMs pay attention to as the
multi-turn conversation progresses. As a reminder, the summary task
involves a user introducing new documents at each turn. The focus of our
analysis is therefore to understand whether document introduction order
(across turns) affects the likelihood of the LLM citing a document.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

In Figure <a href="#A6.F10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 10 ‣ F.3 Over-adjust based on Last Turn of Conversation ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">10</span></a>, we plot the the results of
our analysis. Each row corresponds to the analysis of summaries
generated at a given turn in the sharded simulation. At turn 1 (top
row), 96% of the cited documents were introduced in the first turn. The
missing 4% correspond to hallucinated citation to documents that were
not introduced, and explains why none of the rows’ distribution sum to
100%. At turn two (second row from the top), summaries include citation
in roughly equal proportion for turn-1 and turn-2 documents (i.e., 48%
and 49%).

</div>

<figure id="A6.F10" class="ltx_figure ltx_align_floatright">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x11.png" id="A6.F10.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="831"
height="843" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 10:
</span>Analysis of citation patterns in summaries generated by LLMs with
the <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulation.
At each turn, the LLM generates an updated summary (y-axis), which
includes citations from the documents that have been revealed up to this
turn. Percentages in a row do not add up to 100% due to citation
hallucinations that occur for some models.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A6.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We interpret this to mean that in 2-turn conversations, LLMs pay roughly
equal attention to documents in either turn. Analysis of summaries
generated in turns 3-8 of sharded simulations reveal an imbalance in the
documents the LLM cites to. In eighth-turn summaries, 20% of citations
are to documents introduced in turn 8, compared to 8% from turn 2 and 3
(150% difference). At a high-level, as the conversation progresses, LLMs
are most likely to cite either documents in the first or last turns, and
less likely to cite documents introduced in intermediary (middle) turns.
This finding mirrors findings of a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">loss-in-the-middle</span>
phenomena of LLMs paying more attention to documents at the start or end
of their provided context, at the cost of middle-context content
\[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>,
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\]. In short, we observe that
the lost-in-the-middle phenomena occurs not only in single-turn
long-context settings, but also in multi-turn conversation. We name this
phenomenon
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">loss-in-middle-turns</span>.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We note that the analysis presented in
Figure <a href="#A6.F10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 10 ‣ F.3 Over-adjust based on Last Turn of Conversation ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">10</span></a> averages numbers across the
15 LLMs included in our main experiment. Even though we observe some
loss-in-middle-turns in all models, the magnitude of the effect varies
across models, typically with more performant models having a more muted
effect, showing they have better capabilities of handling attribution
across multiple turns of context. We do not include model-specific
analyses in this work and leave it for future work.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A6.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">F.4 </span>Overly-verbose Assistant Responses

class="ltx_text" style="background-color:#EBF1FB;">42.5</span></td>
</tr>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 7: </span>Averaged
performance (<span class="math inline">$\overline{P}$</span>) of LLMs on
the six experimental tasks, arranged based on model relative verbosity
(length of response). Performance degrades when models generate longer
responses on five of the six tasks.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A6.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

When simulating multiple conversations based on a common instruction, we
observe variation in responses, particularly in the length of the
response generated by the LLM. To understand how verbosity (length of a
response) affects model performance, we perform a verbosity analysis.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

One difficulty with assessing verbosity is that different tasks and
instructions might require different levels of verbosity. For example,
generating a Python function likely requires a longer than generating an
SQL query. In order to regularize for task-specific variations, we
assign a <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">verbosity tag</span>
calculated for each (LLM, instruction) tuple. For each simulated sharded
conversation involving an LLM on an instruction, we calculate the
average length of the per-turn response (number of total characters in
assistant responses divided by number of turns). We then bin
conversations into quintiles according to this metric. More
specifically, since we simulated $`N=10`$ conversations for each (model,
instruction) pair, we assign 2 simulations per quintile, which we name:
shortest, short, median, long, and longest. We then calculate averaged
performance ($`\overline{P}`$) on the six experimental tasks, arranged
based on this verbosity tag. Results are summarized in
Table <a href="#A6.T7" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 7 ‣ F.4 Overly-verbose Assistant Responses ‣ Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

On five of the six tasks, performance is 10-50% higher in simulated
conversations with shortest response length, compared to conversations
with longest response length. As assistant responses get longer (left to
right in the Table), performances gradually drop. The Actions task is
the only task where such an effect is not observed, and where shortest
response length from the assistant is detrimental.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Predominantly however, models achieve higher performance when they
generate shorter responses. We hypothesize that deterioration due to
over-verbosity is due to longer responses typically containing more
assumptions or hypotheses from the assistant, which can lead to
confusion in following turns. On the other hand, short turns tend to be
focused (e.g, a single clarification question), and keep the
conversation on track.

</div>

<div id="A6.SS4.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Deterioration due to over-verbosity is note-worthy, as besides
deteriorating underlying model performance, longer responses also take
longer for users to read, which is undesirable. The finding therefore
indicates that longer LLM responses are bad both for models and
end-users.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A7" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix G </span>Assistant Response Categorization

<div id="A7.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We categorize each assistant response into one of the seven categories
to capture the answer attempt and evaluate if that is the case, as well
as to understand the model behavior tendency. Herlihy et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\] defined seven turn
categories for LLM responses and classified them using LLM, uncovering
that GPT-4 prefers answering directly even when the query is
underspecified. Motivated by this study, we similarly define seven
response categories which we list in
Table <a href="#A7.T8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 8 ‣ Appendix G Assistant Response Categorization ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>, together with example
responses. Key differences are discussion and answer attempt; we
observed many responses containing large body of text formulating the
question in our preliminary experiments, which led to redefining
“Miscellaneous” from \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\]
into “Discussion” in our experiment. “Direct Response” in
\[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\] corresponds to our
“Answer Attempt.”

</div>

<figure id="A7.T8" class="ltx_table">
<table class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_align_middle">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 33%" />
<col style="width: 33%" />
<col style="width: 33%" />
</colgroup>

<div id="A8" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix H </span>Model Access

<div id="A8.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We accessed models that were used in the experiments from various
vendors. The short form names we used throughout the paper, the
corresponding versions, and the providers are summarized in
Table <a href="#A8.T9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 9 ‣ Appendix H Model Access ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a>. Except for the exploration
with various temperatures (Section <a href="#S7.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.2 Implications for LLM Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.2</span></a>), we set the temperature to
$`T=1.0`$ and used the default values for the rest of configurable
hyperparameters. We set the maximum response length to 1,000 tokens for
all models, and did not observe models exceeding this limit frequently
when generating responses. For thinking models (o3, Deepseek-R1), we
increased the limit to 10,000 tokens to account for the additional
test-time compute (thinking tokens).

</div>
