<div id="A9" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix I </span>Task-specific Implementation details

<div id="A9.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We provide task implementation details. For each task, we specify: (1)
the selection of original single-turn fully-specified instruction, (2)
the evaluation metric that was repurposed from the original dataset, (3)
and what the initial system messages consists of (if any).

</div>

<div id="A9.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.1 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/python.png" id="A9.SS1.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Code

<div id="A9.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Code instructions are sourced from a combination of
HumanEval \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">10</a>\], a dataset of
164 basic Python programming problems given the function header and the
docstring that specifies the problem, and
LiveCodeBench \[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\], an
evolving dataset of Python algorithmic challenges. In particular, we
source from the “call-based” problem subset in LiveCodeBench v5, with
the difficulty of either “Easy” and “Medium”, to align the solution
formats between the two sources.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We first sharded all HumanEval problems following the protocol mentioned
in Appendix <a href="#A3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix C Semi-Automatic Sharding Process ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C</span></a>, obtaining 45 high quality
sets of shards that meet the criteria. The rest of the dataset were
discarded because of being simplistic, leaving little room to construct
sufficient number of shards for a problem. Subsequently, we shuffled and
sharded the aforementioned subset from LiveCodeBench until obtaining 100
valid sharded instructions.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We follow the original prompts used by the benchmark authors as much as
possible for the single-turn
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span>) evaluation.
Specifically, <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span>
prompt from HumanEval includes the function header and the docstring
provided as <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">prompt</span> in
HumanEval dataset, and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Full</span> &
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Concat</span> from
LiveCodeBench includes
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">starter_code</span>
consisting of the function signature.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Both HumanEval- and LiveCodeBench-derived problems come with test cases
which we use to compute the functional accuracy of the answer attempt by
the LLMs. We re-use the evaluation codebase maintained by Jain et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>\], which (1) wraps the
candidate function in a test module, (2) execute given the inputs, and
(3) checks the equivalence of the output from the expected output, with
a default timeout set to prevent the evaluator from getting trapped
during evaluation (<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">e.g.</span>,
brute-force implementation may not pass under the set time budget). In
case when multiple code blocks are present in a response, the answer
extraction module selects the last function definition in the last
markdown code block.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.2 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/database.png" id="A9.SS2.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Database

<div id="A9.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Database instructions are sourced from the validation portion of the
Spider dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">86</a>\]. We note
that though a more recent version of Spider has been released (Spider
2.0 \[<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">44</a>\]), the instructions
in the second iteration are more advanced and represent less typical
database use, and we select instructions from the more realistic Spider
1.0.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The authors of Spider categorized queries into four levels of difficulty
(EASY, MEDIUM, HARD, XHARD), based on the syntax complexity of a
reference SQL query. We filtered out queries of EASY complexity, as they
tended to yield fewer than three shards when processed. The rest of the
433 natural language queries in Spider were gradually sharded until
reaching a total of 107 valid sharded instructions.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each original instruction in Spider supplies a database schema,
represented in SQL as a series of table schema (i.e., each define a
series of columns including name, type, and optional index). We include
the database schema as part of the system message (i.e., prior to the
first turn of conversation), and informing the LLM that users will
provide natural-language queries that must be answered using a database
with the provided schema.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each original instruction in Spider is paired with a reference SQL
solution. We follow Zhong et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib90" class="ltx_ref">90</a>\] for the evaluation
methodology. For a given original instruction, the candidate and
reference SQL queries are executed on a fixed set of databases, and
exact match of the results on all databases is required to mark the
candidate as successful (Score = 100). If a discrepancy is observed on
any test database, the candidate is incorrect (Score = 0). One
limitation of SQL execution is that false positives can occur: two
queries can return the same output on a given database, even when they
are not semantically equivalent. Zhong et al.
\[<a href="#bib.bib90" class="ltx_ref">90</a>\] found that by evaluating
on an increased number of databases, false positives become negligible.
Finally, any invalid candidate that does not successfully execute (e.g.,
syntax error) is considered incorrect (Score = 0).

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.3 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/apis.png" id="A9.SS3.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Actions

<div id="A9.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Actions instructions are sourced from the released test portion of
the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard V3 (BFCL)
\[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref">85</a>\]. BFCL V3 consists of
three sub-genre of instructions: (1) Parallel, (2) Multiple, and (3)
Multiple-Parallel. Initial experimentation with the sub-genres
identified Parallel as the most suited for sharding, as Parallel
instructions specify multiple subtasks that should be used and combined
into a single action that accomplishes the entirety of the instruction.
We shuffled all the BFCL V3 Parallel instructions, and sharded gradually
until we obtained 105 valid sharded instructions.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We note that though a more recent iteration of BFCL includes multi-turn
instructions, it differs from sharding experiments as it does not
involve underspecification, with each turn having an independent
intermediate solution (which we call episodic multi-turn conversations).
Our implementation in comparison shards original instructions allowing
us to simulate multi-turn underspecified conversations for this task
setting. The Background section (Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Background and Related Work ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) discusses the relationship
between episodic and underspecified multi-turn conversation more
in-depth.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each instruction in BFCL comes with tool set documentation, a JSON
object that specifies the set of available actions (APIs) for the
assistant to complete user instructions. We include the tool set
documentation as part of the system message, along with a message
indicating that user queries will require the use of the provided tools
to be completed.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each instruction in BFCL comes with a reference answer, consisting of
the API calls that should be called to accomplish the user instruction.
The maintainers of BFCL have released an evaluation toolkit that
assesses semantic equivalence between a candidate answer and the
reference answer. We leverage the official evaluation toolkit, assigning
a score of S=100 for candidate answers that are considered semantically
equivalent to the reference answer, and a score of S=0 otherwise. When
the evaluation toolkit is not able to parse a candidate answer (e.g., a
syntax error), the candidate is considered incorrect (S=0).

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.4 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/math.png" id="A9.SS4.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Math

<div id="A9.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Math instructions are sourced from the “main” portion of the GSM8K
dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>\]. We did not
perform a filter on the original 8,700 instructions. We shuffled the
instructions and sharded incrementally until we obtained 103 valid
sharded instructions. Each GSM8K is paired with a numerical reference
answer. We used the official toolkit released alongside GSM8K to
standardize numerical answers (i.e., strip formatting, etc.).
Standardized candidate numerical answers can then be compared through
exact match to the reference answer. If the toolkit detects a match, the
candidate answer is considered correct (Score=100), and incorrect
otherwise (Score = 0). A short, single-sentence system prompt is used to
indicate to the assistant that it will be solving mathematical problems.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.5 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/data2text.png" id="A9.SS5.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Data-to-Text

<div id="A9.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Data-to-Text instructions are based on instructions in the released
test set ToTTo dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">59</a>\].
In ToTTo, fully-specified instructions have the following information
elements: (1) a HTML-formatted table extracted from a Wikipedia page,
(2) a subset of cells in the table that have been
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">highlighted</span>, (3) the name
of the Wikipedia page that included the Table, (4) the name of the
Section in the Wikipedia page that included the Table. Given these
elements, the task objective is to generate a caption for the Table
specifically focusing on the highlighted cells and considering the
available meta-data. Instructions were shuffled and sharded
incrementally until we obtained 120 valid sharded instructions.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS5.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

For each instruction, we generate sharded instructions by assigning
different information elements to individual shards. The first shard
consists of the initial HTML-formatted table without highlighting. The
second shard provides an updated table with the highlighting present,
the third shard provides the Wikipedia page name, the fourth shard
provides the Wikipedia Section name. Finally, a fifth shard provides a
fixed set of 10 randomly-selected example captions from the training set
of the ToTTo dataset.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS5.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Each instruction in ToTTo is assigned one to three reference captions
that were collected by authors of the original dataset. Evaluation on a
candidate caption calculates the BLEU score
\[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\] between the candidate
and the set of available references, following the evaluation
methodology from the original paper.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS5.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Data-to-Text is a refinement task; at each turn, the model is
provided an additional shard of information, and is explicitly told to
update its response considering all the information provided so far. As
a refinement task, assistant responses at each turn are automatically
categorized as answer attempts, and the extracted answer is considered
to be the entire response. The system instruction informs the model that
its response should consist solely of a table caption, without
additional text (such as intro, outro, or politeness wording).

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS6" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.6 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/summary.png" id="A9.SS6.m1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14" height="14"
alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Summary

<div id="A9.SS6.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Summary instructions are based on samples of the Summary of a
Haystack dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\]. We
reuse the entire instructions from Summary of a Haystack to produce 92
sharded instructions. The original instructions each consist of a
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">haystack</span> – 100 documents
for a total of 100,000 tokens of content – and a user query. The goal of
the task is to generate a bullet-point-formatted summary of the
query-relevant insights that occur in the collection of documents, and
use citation to attribute information in each of the bullet points back
to the source documents.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS6.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The original setting of the Summary of a Haystack purposefully includes
a large amount of redundancy (each insight is repeated across at least 6
documents) to evaluate LLMs’ ability to thoroughly cite sources.
However, we simplify the task for the multi-turn setting, as the
100,000-token haystacks restrict the variety of models we can evaluate.
We instead follow subsequent work in selecting smaller Haystacks
(“mini-Haystacks”) \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">3</a>\].
Mini-Haystacks consist of 20 documents and ensure that each reference
insight is repeated across three documents. For each instruction, we
produce ten shards by randomly assigning two documents per shard. The
initial shard further specifies high-level task instruction, by
specifying the user query, the expected bullet-point format, with a
formatted citation.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS6.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Summary of a Haystack relies on an LLM-based metric (Joint Score) to
compute the quality of the summary in terms of both the relevance of the
candidate bullet points (coverage) and the quality of the generated
attribution within the bullet points (citation). The authors note that
the metric is recall-based, such that longer summaries are likely to
score higher than shorter ones. To account for length bias, the original
task instructs models to generate summaries of at most 300 words, which
we include in our experiments as well. Specifically, models are
instructed in all settings to generate summaries of up to 300 words. We
observed that in multi-turn settings, models often
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">forget</span> this instruction,
leading to non-adherence to the instruction. To avoid penalizing models
that correctly remain within the 300-word limit, we truncate summaries
that go beyond the limit, removing words in equal proportion from
summary bullet points, such that evaluated summaries all respect the
300-word limit. We note that this tendency for LLMs to go beyond is
further discussed in Appendix <a href="#A6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix F Qualitative Analyses of Simulation Logs ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">F</span></a>, where we observe that across
tasks, model answer attempts get “bloated” over turns of conversations.
In single-turn settings (full, concat), LLMs largely respect the
300-word length limit.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS6.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The summary task is a refinement task. Assistant responses at each turn
are automatically categorized as answer attempts, and the entire
response is considered to be the extracted answer.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A9.SS7" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">I.7 </span> <span class="ltx_inline-block ltx_markedasmath ltx_align_bottom"> <span class="ltx_p"><img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/icons/translation.png"
id="A9.SS7.m1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_square" width="14"
height="14" alt="[Uncaptioned image]" /></span> </span> Translation

<div id="A9.SS7.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The Translation instructions were collected from the WMT 2019 task on
document-level translation
\[<a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>\]. Specifically, we
selected 30 documents German-English pairs. Document pairs are aligned
at the sentence level (i.e., English and German documents in a pair have
the same number of sentences). We truncated the selected pairs to their
first ten sentences, and sharded the document instruction such that each
shard would introduce exactly two sentences from the document, for a
total of five shards. We provided shards in German, and the task
consisted in translating into English (i.e., German$`\to`$English).
Hence, Shard 1 introduces the first two German sentences, Shard 2
introduces German sentences 3-4, etc. In the sharded setting, the task
requires the LLM to translate the document with all the provided
sentences so far. In the full settings, the LLM is provided the entire
document (10 sentences) in the first turn. In the concat setting, the
LLM is also provided all sentences in the first turn, but separated into
the shards (two sentences at a time).

</div>

<div id="A9.SS7.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In initial experiments, we experimented with other sharding strategies,
including breaking shards at a specific number of words (rather than
sentence boundary), and increasing the length of documents (from 10 to
20 sentences), without observing significant differences in results.
This led us to adopt the setting we describe: sharding every two
sentences, and truncating at 10-sentences.

</div>

<div id="A9.SS7.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We evaluated performance with the BLEU
metric\[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\], the standard
metric for translation tasks, which was used as well in the original WMT
2019 competition.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A10" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix J </span>Example Simulated Conversation

<figure id="A10.F11" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2505.06120/assets/x12.png" id="A10.F11.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="415" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 11:
</span>Example simulated multi-turn conversation for the Math task. This
conversation simulation was with assistant model Llama3.1-8B-Instruct.
The sharded instruction consists of six shards. The correct answer to
the instruction is 85,000 calories.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A10.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Figure <a href="#A10.F11" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 11 ‣ Appendix J Example Simulated Conversation ‣ Appendices ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">11</span></a> provides an example
conversation that was simulated during our experiments in the sharded
setting. The simulation was conducted on the Math task, with a 6-shard
instruction, and using the Llama3.1-8B-Instruct as the assistant. This
conversation illustrates the following properties described in the rest
of the paper: (1) the LLM makes assumptions early in the conversation
(in Turn 1, describing four pastries that are irrelevant), (2) although
it correctly interprets user-provided information, it also unnecessarily
updates the information for assumptions it made (Turn 4), (3) this leads
to unnecessary complexity, and the model ultimately forgets that the
initial instruction was to calculate total calorie count, and returns
only half of the calculation (just for Mini Blueberry Muffin). In short,
this conversation illustrates the lost in conversation phenomenon: when
the user instruction is underspecified (Turns 1-4), the LLM makes
assumptions that detract from the conversation and lead to incorrect or
incomplete answers.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A11" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix K </span>Gradual Sharding Implementation

<div id="A11.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To evaluate the effect of instruction granularity on performance
degradations, we conducted the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">gradual sharding
experiment</span>.

</div>

<div id="A11.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We selected sharded instructions that had exactly eight shards, leading
to a total of eight instructions across three tasks (Code, Math,
Data-to-Text). We then leveraged an LLM (GPT-4o) to expand each
instruction into 7 variants with differing number of shards. The LLM was
instructed to <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">merge</span> the
original sharded instruction into a smaller sharded instruction with two
to seven shards. The instruction authorized minor rephrasing to allow
for individual shards to be fluent, but encouraged the LLM to remain as
close as possible to the original instruction in wording.

</div>

<div id="A11.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

As such, each of the original instruction can be paired to: (1) a concat
instruction (one-shard), and (2) 7 sharded instructions, ranging from
two to eight shards. Applying this method to the 31 instructions yields
a total of 248 instructions, with an equal number for the number of
shards (from 1 to 8) and on the identical underlying problems.

</div>

<div id="A11.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We ran simulations using the 248 instructions, simulating 10
conversations per instruction and model for two models: GPT-4o and
GPT-4o-mini. Findings of the gradual sharding experiment are described
in Section <a href="#S6.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="6.3 Gradual Sharding Experiment ‣ 6 Results ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.3</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A12" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix L </span>Temperature Experiment Implementation

<div id="A12.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To evaluate the effect of temperature on aptitude and reliability of
LLMs in single- and multi-turn settings, we conducted the following
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">temperature experiment</span>.

</div>

<div id="A12.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We selected 10 instructions from each of four tasks: Code, Database,
Actions, and Math (for a total of 40). We ran experiments with two
models (GPT-4o and GPT-4o-mini). For each instruction and each
temperature combination, we conducted simulations for three conversation
settings: full, concat, and sharded. For each conversation setting, we
varied temperature parameters to three values: 0.0, 0.5, and 1.0. For
the full and concat setings, this corresponds to three temperature
combinations (as only the assistant temperature can be modified),
whereas there are a total of nine combinations for the sharded setting,
as both the assistant and user temperature is varied.

</div>

<div id="A12.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We chose to increase the number of simulations to 20 runs per condition
(compared to 10 in the main experiment), as the focus of the experiment
is to measure variations in model aptitude and reliability, and added
simulation runs lead to better percentile estimates used in calculating
metrics. This added requirement was not computationally expensive as the
temperature experiment involved a limited number of models (2 vs. 15)
and instructions (40 vs. 600) in comparison to our main experiment.

</div>

<div id="A12.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Findings of the experiments are described in
Section <a href="#S7.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="7.2 Implications for LLM Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A13" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix M </span>Recap & Snowball Experiment Implementation

<div id="A13.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We leverage <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span>
conversation logs to simulate
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> setting, since
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> only differs from
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> in terms of an
additional recapitulation turn that gathers all the previous user
utterances. This implementation also allows us to directly compare the
effect of the approach against the
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> results.
Specifically, for each
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Sharded</span> simulation run,
we appended the “recap” turn and run the simulation one more turn. Since
it requires stacking the past turns every turn, we simulate the entire
conversations from scratch for
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span> simulations.
The prompt concatenates the previous turn user utterances as bullet
points, followed by the text for the current turn:
`Just to reiterate:\n - [past utterance 1]\n- [past utterance 2]\n\n Also,\n[current utterance]`.
We note that what is accumulated for both
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Recap</span> and
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Snowball</span> are verbalized
utterances from the user simulator, not the original shards themselves.
For both simulation settings, we run $`N=10`$ simulations on all of the
sharded instructions on four tasks (Code, Database, Math, Actions) and
report the mean of averaged performance over the tasks, which is shown
in Table <a href="#S7.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 7.1 Implications for System and Agent Builders ‣ 7 Implications ‣ LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A14" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix N </span>On obtaining deterministic outputs from LLMs

<div id="A14.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

As we demonstrated in our experimental results, setting the temperatures
to zero still leads to high unreliability, due to compounding effect of
subtle non-determinism over tokens and turns.

</div>

<div id="A14.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In theory, greedy decoding
(<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">i.e.</span>, $`T=0`$) will
always pick the argmax over the vocabulary distribution. However, it is
reported that hardware limitations on floating point operations cause
slightly different intermediate values, which results in a ripple effect
of larger value changes and therefore different tokens being selected.

</div>

<div id="A14.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Notable model providers acknowledge the non-determinism implicitly or
explicitly; Anthropic recommends sampling multiple times to
cross-validate output consistency,<span id="footnote3"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>3</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>3</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">3</span><a
href="https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/test-and-evaluate/strengthen-guardrails/reduce-hallucinations"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/test-and-evaluate/strengthen-guardrails/reduce-hallucinations</a>.</span></span></span>
Google also highlights that their model outputs are
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">mostly</span>
deterministic,<span id="footnote4"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>4</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>4</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">4</span><a
href="https://cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/learn/prompts/adjust-parameter-values#temperature"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/learn/prompts/adjust-parameter-values#temperature</a>.</span></span></span>
and OpenAI recommends setting
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">seed</span> parameter to
further reduce the non-determinism.<span id="footnote5"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>5</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>5</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">5</span><a
href="https://platform.openai.com/docs/advanced-usage#reproducible-outputs"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://platform.openai.com/docs/advanced-usage#reproducible-outputs</a>.</span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="A14.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Nevertheless, we caution users that multi-turn conversations can be
increasingly unreliable owing to divergent LLM responses.

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

</div>

<div id="A15" class="section ltx_appendix">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix O </span>Prompts

<div id="A15.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">O.1 </span>Sharding

<div id="A15.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We show the prompts for the sharding process below, using Math as an
example task. Double-bracketed terms are placeholders that get replaced
with the actual data. Other tasks share the same outline with different
exemplars and rules to enforce stable outputs. We refer the readers to
the GitHub repository for the exact prompts on other tasks.

</div>

<div id="A15.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">O.2 </span>Experiments

<div id="A15.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The experiments involve several LLM calls with specific prompts to
simulate the conversation, which we list below. We refer readers to the
GitHub repository for how they are incorporated.

</div>
